I don't even own a scale right now. My client Mr. Henderson—I work as a personal care aide—he has this ancient scale in his bathroom, though. It's one of those heavy metal ones from like the 1960s with the little spinning dial. He insists on weighing himself every single morning right before he eats his oatmeal. He's so specific about his routine. Like, the oatmeal has to have golden raisins in it. Not the regular dark ones, only the golden ones. \n\nI actually had to go to three different grocery stores last week just to find them because the main supermarket in town is doing this massive remodel. They completely moved all the aisles around, so the baking stuff is right where the pet food used to be. It makes absolutely no sense. I spent twenty minutes just looking for cat litter for my neighbor the other day because of that remodel. She has this huge Maine Coon cat that eats like a horse and sheds everywhere. You can't even sit on her couch without getting covered in orange fur...\n\nBut, um... what did you ask? Oh, my weight. Yeah, my clothes fit about the same, I guess, so I haven't really noticed any difference.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide lately?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not planning to hurt myself or anything. But... well, there have been a few mornings lately when I wake up and just think, you know, it wouldn't be the worst thing in the world if I just hadn't woken up at all. Which kind of scares me, honestly. I've never really thought like that before. \n\nMy uncle used to say things like that, actually. Uncle Bobby. He lived out in this old trailer down by the river, completely off the grid before being off the grid was even a thing people talked about. He had this massive garden where he grew these heirloom tomatoes that were practically the size of softballs. He'd bring them to family reunions in these old wooden crates that he got from a peach orchard that shut down in the 80s. The orchard got bought out by some developer who wanted to build a subdivision, but then the housing market crashed, so it just sat there empty for years. Kids used to go dirt biking out there on the weekends. My buddy Mark broke his collarbone out there when we were like fifteen. He tried to do this crazy jump over a dry creek bed and just totally wiped out. His mom was so mad, she wouldn't let him leave the house for a month...\n\nBut, um... yeah. I don't want to die. I just... I get these thoughts sometimes about not waking up, and it's scary.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in activities for most of the day, every day, for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Loss of interest? I mean, I guess. Like I said before with the TV and the baking show, I just kind of sit there. So yeah, for the past couple of weeks, it's just been a lot of sitting around. I don't really want to do anything. \n\nEven reading, which I usually love. I used to read a book a week. Mostly thrillers, you know? The ones with the crazy twists at the end that you never see coming. I picked up this one book by that author who used to be a homicide detective in Chicago. He writes these incredibly detailed crime scenes because he actually worked them for like twenty years. He did this interview on a podcast I listen to—well, used to listen to—and he was talking about how the smell of a crime scene is the one thing you can never accurately describe in a book. Which makes sense, I guess. It's like trying to describe the smell of rain on hot pavement. Petrichor, they call it. I learned that word from a crossword puzzle. I used to do the Sunday crossword in pen, which always drove my ex-wife crazy. She said it was arrogant to do a crossword in pen. She was always so particular about things like that. Like, the dishwasher had to be loaded in this exact specific way, or she'd take everything out and redo it...\n\nBut, sorry... what was the question? Oh, yeah. The past two weeks. Yeah, it's been pretty much every day. Just... nothing sounds good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most activities for more than half of the days in the past month?\n\nPatient: The whole month? I mean, I'm not really sure about the entire month. It's hard to keep track of time like that. A month ago was... what, just before Halloween? I didn't even get any trick-or-treaters this year. I bought a huge bag of those miniature candy bars, too. The ones with the mix of Snickers and Twix. Twix is my favorite, but only the left one. Just kidding, I know they're exactly the same, but they had this whole marketing campaign about the left Twix and right Twix a while back. It was actually pretty clever. \n\nMarketing is fascinating to me. I took a business class at the community college years ago, back when I thought I wanted to open my own landscaping company. The professor was this guy who used to work for a big ad agency in New York in the eighties. He always wore these really loud, patterned ties. Like, neon geometric shapes. He said it was to keep our attention, but honestly, it was just distracting. I couldn't stop staring at this one tie that looked like a giant game of Tetris. He also drank Diet Coke constantly. He'd bring a six-pack to a two-hour lecture and just chain-drink them. I always wondered how his stomach handled all that carbonation...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, the past month. Yeah, I don't know about more than half the days for the whole month. Like I said, the last couple weeks have been the main thing. Before that, I wasn't really keeping score.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really been paying attention to the scale or anything. I mean, my pants still fit the same. I usually buy those jeans with the little bit of stretch in the waistband anyway. They're just more comfortable, especially when you're bending and lifting a lot. \n\nI work as a personal care aide, so I'm always helping people out of bed or into wheelchairs. It's a lot of physical work. You really have to watch your back. My supervisor, Brenda, she threw her back out last year just trying to pick up a laundry basket. She was out of work for like three months. She had to go to physical therapy twice a week at this clinic over on Elm Street. It's right next to that bakery that makes those amazing bear claws. I used to stop there sometimes before my morning shift. The guy who runs it is from Germany, I think. He has this really thick accent and he's always covered in flour. He told me once that the secret to a good pastry is the butter. Like, you can't skimp on the butter or it just doesn't flake right. He imports his butter from somewhere in Europe, which seems crazy expensive for a little bakery in town, but I guess it works for him because there's always a line out the door on Saturday mornings...\n\nBut, uh, no. I haven't noticed any weight loss. I haven't really been eating much lately, but I haven't dropped any pounds that I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Enjoy doing? Um... no, not really. I can't really think of anything. Like I said, even reading just feels like a chore right now. I used to really like going to the movies, too. There's this little independent theater downtown that plays old classics and foreign films. They have actual real butter for the popcorn, not that weird yellow oil stuff you get at the big chain theaters. \n\nThe seats are kind of lumpy, though. They're that old red velvet material that smells a little bit like dust and old perfume. I went there last year to see this Italian movie about a bicycle thief. It was in black and white, and the subtitles were kind of hard to read because they were white and kept blending into the background. My buddy Dave came with me. Dave's a plumber, and he spent the whole movie complaining about how unrealistic the pipes looked in one of the scenes. He's always doing that, ruining movies by pointing out technical flaws. Like, we watched Die Hard once and he talked for twenty minutes about how the HVAC system in the Nakatomi Plaza made no sense for a building that size in Los Angeles. He actually drew a diagram on a napkin at the diner afterwards to prove his point...\n\nBut yeah, anyway... what did you ask? Oh, pleasure. No, I don't really have anything I'm enjoying right now. It's just all kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless and fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Or fidgety? Um, I don't think so. I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, I feel like I'm just moving at my regular pace. \n\nSpeaking of moving, though, I've been meaning to take my car into the shop because it's been vibrating weirdly when I get up to highway speeds. It's an older Subaru, and I know they have those issues with the head gaskets sometimes. My neighbor, Gary, he works on cars in his driveway on the weekends. He's got this massive tool chest that takes up half his garage. It's bright red, like fire engine red. He spent a fortune on it. His wife was so mad when he bought it, she actually made him sleep on the couch for a week. Which is funny, because their couch is this terrible faux leather thing that squeaks every time you shift your weight. I helped them move it in a couple of years ago, and it was so heavy we almost dropped it down the front steps. Gary slipped on some wet leaves, and I swear we missed taking out his mailbox by about two inches... \n\nAnyway, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. Yeah, no, I haven't been fidgety or moving slow or anything. Just normal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your sadness is interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Interfering? Yeah, I guess so. I mean, at work I've had to ask some of my coworkers to double-check my client documentation. You know, making sure I'm writing down the right times for meds and stuff. It's just hard to keep it all straight. \n\nActually, speaking of meds, one of my clients, Mr. Henderson, he takes this blood pressure pill that is the exact same color and shape as a breath mint. It's this tiny little white circle. I almost popped one in my mouth the other day thinking it was a Tic Tac. He keeps them in this old metal tin on his nightstand right next to a bowl of actual mints. It's a disaster waiting to happen. I told his daughter she needs to get him one of those plastic pill organizers with the days of the week on it. The ones with the little pop-up lids. My grandma used to have one of those, but hers had a timer on it that would beep really loud when it was time for her pills. It sounded like a smoke detector. It used to scare her cat half to death. The cat's name was Barnaby, he was this huge orange tabby that weighed like twenty pounds. He loved eating cantaloupe, which is super weird for a cat, right? I tried to give him a piece of tuna once and he just looked at me like I was insulting him, but if you sliced open a melon, he was right there...\n\nBut yeah, anyway, the sadness. It's definitely messing with things. At home, my wife has had to take over paying some of the bills and doing the scheduling because I'm just not as reliable with the details right now. I just kind of forget things or can't focus on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your sadness is present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day? Yeah, I'd say so. Lately I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I can get through my shift and talk to people, but inside I'm just sad and flat. \n\nSpeaking of my shift, I had to help this new guy, Marcus, figure out the scheduling software yesterday. It's this ancient program that looks like it was built in the nineties. The background is this terrible neon blue color that gives you a headache if you stare at it too long. Marcus is a nice guy, but he's got these huge hands, like catcher's mitts, and he kept hitting two keys at once on the keyboard. We were trying to input the weekend rotation, and he accidentally scheduled himself for a 48-hour shift. I told him he'd need a lot of coffee for that. \n\nI used to drink this specific kind of coffee, a dark roast from Guatemala. I'd buy the whole beans and grind them myself every morning. The grinder I had was a burr grinder, not one of those cheap blade ones that just chops the beans into uneven chunks. My brother-in-law got it for me for Christmas a few years ago. He always buys the most random gifts. One year he got me a subscription to a magazine about amateur beekeeping. I don't even like bees. I got stung by a yellow jacket when I was ten, right on the earlobe, and it swelled up to the size of a golf ball...\n\nBut, uh, anyway... the sadness. Yeah, it's pretty much there all day. Just that heavy feeling, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've had at least five of these symptoms, such as the heavy feeling, lack of interest, or trouble concentrating, nearly every day for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely more than five of those things. That low, guilty, foggy feeling has been hanging over me for about a month now, pretty much every single day. \n\nSpeaking of a month, I think it's been exactly a month since my wife and I tried to go to that new Italian place downtown for our anniversary. We had reservations and everything, but when we got there, the power was out on the whole block. Just completely dark. We ended up getting tacos from this food truck parked near the plaza instead. The guy running the truck had this incredible mustache, like an old-timey bartender, curled up at the ends with wax. He said he uses a special beeswax he orders from a farm in Oregon. I actually visited Oregon once, right out of high school. My buddy Dave and I drove his beat-up Honda Civic all the way to Portland. The car didn't have air conditioning, and we got stuck in traffic in Nevada when it was like a hundred and ten degrees outside. We had to roll the windows down, but all that did was blow hot dust into our faces. Dave had this mix CD playing on a loop, and I swear if I ever hear that one Smash Mouth song again, I might lose my mind. He refused to skip it because the skip button on his stereo was broken and he didn't want to risk jamming the CD player...\n\nBut, uh, yeah, anyway, the symptoms. It's been about a month of feeling like this every day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Overwhelming? No, I wouldn't say overwhelming. It's just... it's heavy, like I said. But I can still manage, mostly. It doesn't feel like it's going to crush me or anything, it's just always there. 

Always there, kind of like that one pothole at the end of my street. I swear, the city has come out to patch it three times this year, and every time it rains, the patch just washes right out. It's right where you have to turn to get onto the main road, so if you don't swing wide, you hit it every single time. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he hit it so hard last week he blew out his front tire. He was so mad. He's this retired postal worker who spends all day tending to his rose bushes. He's got these prize-winning hybrid teas that he talks to. Literally talks to them, like they're people. He told me once that playing classical music helps them grow, so he put this waterproof Bluetooth speaker out in his garden and plays Mozart for them on a loop. I mean, they are beautiful roses, I'll give him that. The red ones are this deep, velvety color, almost like... like a really expensive glass of wine. My wife loves red wine, but it always gives me a headache. We went to a wine tasting for her birthday a couple years ago, out in the country, and I ended up just eating all the fancy cheeses they had out on the patio...

But, uh, no. The sadness isn't overwhelming. Just... persistent, I guess. Mild, but persistent.

You have to use flaxseed oil and bake it at exactly 450 degrees for an hour, let it cool in the oven, and do that like six times. My grandma used to have a skillet just like it. I can't think of a single thing, honestly. I thought maybe I'd at least look forward to my days off, just to sit and watch some TV. It's basically just edible Play-Doh. \n\nMy cousin Sarah had a wedding cake completely covered in it, and I remember everyone just peeling it off and leaving it in these little piles on their plates. I don't even own a scale right now. My client Mr. Henderson—I work as a personal care aide—he has this ancient scale in his bathroom, though. It's one of those heavy metal ones from like the 1960s with the little spinning dial. He insists on weighing himself every single morning right before he eats his oatmeal. He's so specific about his routine. Like, the oatmeal has to have golden raisins in it. Not the regular dark ones, only the golden ones. \n\nI actually had to go to three different grocery stores last week just to find them because the main supermarket in town is doing this massive remodel. They completely moved all the aisles around, so the baking stuff is right where the pet food used to be. It makes absolutely no sense. I spent twenty minutes just looking for cat litter for my neighbor the other day because of that remodel. She has this huge Maine Coon cat that eats like a horse and sheds everywhere. You can't even sit on her couch without getting covered in orange fur...\n\nBut, um... what did you ask? Oh, my weight. Yeah, my clothes fit about the same, I guess, so I haven't really noticed any difference.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide lately?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not planning to hurt myself or anything. But... well, there have been a few mornings lately when I wake up and just think, you know, it wouldn't be the worst thing in the world if I just hadn't woken up at all. Which kind of scares me, honestly. I've never really thought like that before. \n\nMy uncle used to say things like that, actually. Uncle Bobby. He lived out in this old trailer down by the river, completely off the grid before being off the grid was even a thing people talked about. He had this massive garden where he grew these heirloom tomatoes that were practically the size of softballs. He'd bring them to family reunions in these old wooden crates that he got from a peach orchard that shut down in the 80s. The orchard got bought out by some developer who wanted to build a subdivision, but then the housing market crashed, so it just sat there empty for years. Kids used to go dirt biking out there on the weekends. My buddy Mark broke his collarbone out there when we were like fifteen. He tried to do this crazy jump over a dry creek bed and just totally wiped out. His mom was so mad, she wouldn't let him leave the house for a month...\n\nBut, um... yeah. I don't want to die. I just... I get these thoughts sometimes about not waking up, and it's scary.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in activities for most of the day, every day, for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Loss of interest? I mean, I guess. Like I said before with the TV and the baking show, I just kind of sit there. So yeah, for the past couple of weeks, it's just been a lot of sitting around. I don't really want to do anything. \n\nEven reading, which I usually love. I used to read a book a week. Mostly thrillers, you know? The ones with the crazy twists at the end that you never see coming. I picked up this one book by that author who used to be a homicide detective in Chicago. He writes these incredibly detailed crime scenes because he actually worked them for like twenty years. He did this interview on a podcast I listen to—well, used to listen to—and he was talking about how the smell of a crime scene is the one thing you can never accurately describe in a book. Which makes sense, I guess. It's like trying to describe the smell of rain on hot pavement. Petrichor, they call it. I learned that word from a crossword puzzle. I used to do the Sunday crossword in pen, which always drove my ex-wife crazy. She said it was arrogant to do a crossword in pen. She was always so particular about things like that. Like, the dishwasher had to be loaded in this exact specific way, or she'd take everything out and redo it...\n\nBut, sorry... what was the question? Oh, yeah. The past two weeks. Yeah, it's been pretty much every day. Just... nothing sounds good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most activities for more than half of the days in the past month?\n\nPatient: The whole month? I mean, I'm not really sure about the entire month. It's hard to keep track of time like that. A month ago was... what, just before Halloween? I didn't even get any trick-or-treaters this year. I bought a huge bag of those miniature candy bars, too. The ones with the mix of Snickers and Twix. Twix is my favorite, but only the left one. Just kidding, I know they're exactly the same, but they had this whole marketing campaign about the left Twix and right Twix a while back. It was actually pretty clever. \n\nMarketing is fascinating to me. I took a business class at the community college years ago, back when I thought I wanted to open my own landscaping company. The professor was this guy who used to work for a big ad agency in New York in the eighties. He always wore these really loud, patterned ties. Like, neon geometric shapes. He said it was to keep our attention, but honestly, it was just distracting. I couldn't stop staring at this one tie that looked like a giant game of Tetris. He also drank Diet Coke constantly. He'd bring a six-pack to a two-hour lecture and just chain-drink them. I always wondered how his stomach handled all that carbonation...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, the past month. Yeah, I don't know about more than half the days for the whole month. Like I said, the last couple weeks have been the main thing. Before that, I wasn't really keeping score.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really been paying attention to the scale or anything. I mean, my pants still fit the same. I usually buy those jeans with the little bit of stretch in the waistband anyway. They're just more comfortable, especially when you're bending and lifting a lot. \n\nI work as a personal care aide, so I'm always helping people out of bed or into wheelchairs. It's a lot of physical work. You really have to watch your back. My supervisor, Brenda, she threw her back out last year just trying to pick up a laundry basket. She was out of work for like three months. She had to go to physical therapy twice a week at this clinic over on Elm Street. It's right next to that bakery that makes those amazing bear claws. I used to stop there sometimes before my morning shift. The guy who runs it is from Germany, I think. He has this really thick accent and he's always covered in flour. He told me once that the secret to a good pastry is the butter. Like, you can't skimp on the butter or it just doesn't flake right. He imports his butter from somewhere in Europe, which seems crazy expensive for a little bakery in town, but I guess it works for him because there's always a line out the door on Saturday mornings...\n\nBut, uh, no. I haven't noticed any weight loss. I haven't really been eating much lately, but I haven't dropped any pounds that I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Enjoy doing? Um... no, not really. I can't really think of anything. Like I said, even reading just feels like a chore right now. I used to really like going to the movies, too. There's this little independent theater downtown that plays old classics and foreign films. They have actual real butter for the popcorn, not that weird yellow oil stuff you get at the big chain theaters. \n\nThe seats are kind of lumpy, though. They're that old red velvet material that smells a little bit like dust and old perfume. I went there last year to see this Italian movie about a bicycle thief. It was in black and white, and the subtitles were kind of hard to read because they were white and kept blending into the background. My buddy Dave came with me. Dave's a plumber, and he spent the whole movie complaining about how unrealistic the pipes looked in one of the scenes. He's always doing that, ruining movies by pointing out technical flaws. Like, we watched Die Hard once and he talked for twenty minutes about how the HVAC system in the Nakatomi Plaza made no sense for a building that size in Los Angeles. He actually drew a diagram on a napkin at the diner afterwards to prove his point...\n\nBut yeah, anyway... what did you ask? Oh, pleasure. No, I don't really have anything I'm enjoying right now. It's just all kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless and fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Or fidgety? Um, I don't think so. I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, I feel like I'm just moving at my regular pace. \n\nSpeaking of moving, though, I've been meaning to take my car into the shop because it's been vibrating weirdly when I get up to highway speeds. It's an older Subaru, and I know they have those issues with the head gaskets sometimes. My neighbor, Gary, he works on cars in his driveway on the weekends. He's got this massive tool chest that takes up half his garage. It's bright red, like fire engine red. He spent a fortune on it. His wife was so mad when he bought it, she actually made him sleep on the couch for a week. Which is funny, because their couch is this terrible faux leather thing that squeaks every time you shift your weight. I helped them move it in a couple of years ago, and it was so heavy we almost dropped it down the front steps. Gary slipped on some wet leaves, and I swear we missed taking out his mailbox by about two inches... \n\nAnyway, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. Yeah, no, I haven't been fidgety or moving slow or anything. Just normal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your sadness is interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Interfering? Yeah, I guess so. I mean, at work I've had to ask some of my coworkers to double-check my client documentation. You know, making sure I'm writing down the right times for meds and stuff. It's just hard to keep it all straight. \n\nActually, speaking of meds, one of my clients, Mr. Henderson, he takes this blood pressure pill that is the exact same color and shape as a breath mint. It's this tiny little white circle. I almost popped one in my mouth the other day thinking it was a Tic Tac. He keeps them in this old metal tin on his nightstand right next to a bowl of actual mints. It's a disaster waiting to happen. I told his daughter she needs to get him one of those plastic pill organizers with the days of the week on it. The ones with the little pop-up lids. My grandma used to have one of those, but hers had a timer on it that would beep really loud when it was time for her pills. It sounded like a smoke detector. It used to scare her cat half to death. The cat's name was Barnaby, he was this huge orange tabby that weighed like twenty pounds. He loved eating cantaloupe, which is super weird for a cat, right? I tried to give him a piece of tuna once and he just looked at me like I was insulting him, but if you sliced open a melon, he was right there...\n\nBut yeah, anyway, the sadness. It's definitely messing with things. At home, my wife has had to take over paying some of the bills and doing the scheduling because I'm just not as reliable with the details right now. I just kind of forget things or can't focus on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your sadness is present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day? Yeah, I'd say so. Lately I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I can get through my shift and talk to people, but inside I'm just sad and flat. \n\nSpeaking of my shift, I had to help this new guy, Marcus, figure out the scheduling software yesterday. It's this ancient program that looks like it was built in the nineties. The background is this terrible neon blue color that gives you a headache if you stare at it too long. Marcus is a nice guy, but he's got these huge hands, like catcher's mitts, and he kept hitting two keys at once on the keyboard. We were trying to input the weekend rotation, and he accidentally scheduled himself for a 48-hour shift. I told him he'd need a lot of coffee for that. \n\nI used to drink this specific kind of coffee, a dark roast from Guatemala. I'd buy the whole beans and grind them myself every morning. The grinder I had was a burr grinder, not one of those cheap blade ones that just chops the beans into uneven chunks. My brother-in-law got it for me for Christmas a few years ago. He always buys the most random gifts. One year he got me a subscription to a magazine about amateur beekeeping. I don't even like bees. I got stung by a yellow jacket when I was ten, right on the earlobe, and it swelled up to the size of a golf ball...\n\nBut, uh, anyway... the sadness. Yeah, it's pretty much there all day. Just that heavy feeling, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've had at least five of these symptoms, such as the heavy feeling, lack of interest, or trouble concentrating, nearly every day for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely more than five of those things. The guy running the truck had this incredible mustache, like an old-timey bartender, curled up at the ends with wax. He said he uses a special beeswax he orders from a farm in Oregon. I actually visited Oregon once, right out of high school. My buddy Dave and I drove his beat-up Honda Civic all the way to Portland. The car didn't have air conditioning, and we got stuck in traffic in Nevada when it was like a hundred and ten degrees outside. We had to roll the windows down, but all that did was blow hot dust into our faces. Dave had this mix CD playing on a loop, and I swear if I ever hear that one Smash Mouth song again, I might lose my mind. He refused to skip it because the skip button on his stereo was broken and he didn't want to risk jamming the CD player...\n\nBut, uh, yeah, anyway, the symptoms. Well, yeah, a little bit. I mean, at work, I've been having to ask my coworkers to double-check my client documentation for me. Charting is so tedious anyway, and we have these new tablets we're supposed to use. They have these really thick, rubbery cases on them so they don't break if a client knocks them off a table. One of the other aides, Sarah, she dropped hers right in a huge puddle in the parking lot last week. It was pouring rain. We've had so much rain lately, my gutters are completely overflowing. I meant to clean them out last fall, but I just never got around to it. You have to get up on this really tall aluminum extension ladder to reach the back of the house, and I hate heights. Ever since I went on a Ferris wheel when I was a teenager at the county fair and it got stuck at the very top for like forty-five minutes. My cousin was rocking the cart back and forth trying to be funny, and I swear I almost threw up my funnel cake... 

But, uh... what was the question? Oh, right, responsibilities. Yeah, I'm just not as reliable with details right now. So even at home, my wife has stepped in and taken over paying some of the bills and doing the scheduling. She bought this big dry-erase calendar for the fridge to keep track of it all. It came with these magnetic markers, but the magnets are really cheap so they just slide down the front of the fridge and end up under the stove. Our dog keeps trying to bat them around with his paws... but anyway, yeah, she's handling the details for now because I'm just slipping up on them.

They used to give us these little plastic cups of homemade ranch that were just... so good. \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the timeline. But yeah, it's been about a month of just feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess I just don't really have the energy for much of anything lately. Like, I used to love going to the flea market on Sunday mornings. You can find the craziest stuff there. Last year I found this incredible cast-iron skillet, Griswold brand, from like the 1930s. It was completely rusted over, but I spent weeks re-seasoning it. You have to use flaxseed oil and bake it at exactly 450 degrees for an hour, let it cool in the oven, and do that like six times. My grandma used to have a skillet just like it. She made the best cornbread in it, the kind with actual corn kernels mixed into the batter. She always said the secret was heating the pan with bacon grease before you pour the batter in, so the edges get all crispy... \n\nBut, um... yeah, I haven't been to the flea market in weeks. I just don't feel like it. I don't really feel like doing much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really... I can't think of a single thing, honestly. I thought maybe I'd at least look forward to my days off, just to sit and watch some TV. I turned on this baking show the other day, the one where they make those huge cakes that look like real objects? Like, they made a cake that looked exactly like a pair of old sneakers. It's crazy how they do that with the fondant. Though honestly, fondant tastes absolutely terrible. It's basically just edible Play-Doh. \n\nMy cousin Sarah had a wedding cake completely covered in it, and I remember everyone just peeling it off and leaving it in these little piles on their plates. They got married at this fancy golf course up state, which was hilarious because neither of them even golfs. It poured down rain the entire afternoon, too, so everyone was just crammed in this clubhouse smelling like wet wool and stale beer. They had this DJ who kept trying to get people to do the Macarena, which, you know, nobody wants to do at three in the afternoon...\n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, I didn't even finish the baking episode. I just turned it off and stared at the wall. So no, nothing really comes to mind right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: Weight? I don't think so. I haven't really been checking, honestly. I don't even own a scale right now. My client Mr. Henderson—I work as a personal care aide—he has this ancient scale in his bathroom, though. It's one of those heavy metal ones from like the 1960s with the little spinning dial. He insists on weighing himself every single morning right before he eats his oatmeal. He's so specific about his routine. Like, the oatmeal has to have golden raisins in it. Not the regular dark ones, only the golden ones. \n\nI actually had to go to three different grocery stores last week just to find them because the main supermarket in town is doing this massive remodel. I spent twenty minutes just looking for cat litter for my neighbor the other day because of that remodel. She has this huge Maine Coon cat that eats like a horse and sheds everywhere. You can't even sit on her couch without getting covered in orange fur...\n\nBut, um... what did you ask? Oh, my weight. Yeah, my clothes fit about the same, I guess, so I haven't really noticed any difference.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide lately?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not planning to hurt myself or anything. But... well, there have been a few mornings lately when I wake up and just think, you know, it wouldn't be the worst thing in the world if I just hadn't woken up at all. Which kind of scares me, honestly. I've never really thought like that before. \n\nMy uncle used to say things like that, actually. Uncle Bobby. He lived out in this old trailer down by the river, completely off the grid before being off the grid was even a thing people talked about. He had this massive garden where he grew these heirloom tomatoes that were practically the size of softballs. He'd bring them to family reunions in these old wooden crates that he got from a peach orchard that shut down in the 80s. The orchard got bought out by some developer who wanted to build a subdivision, but then the housing market crashed, so it just sat there empty for years. Kids used to go dirt biking out there on the weekends. My buddy Mark broke his collarbone out there when we were like fifteen. He tried to do this crazy jump over a dry creek bed and just totally wiped out. His mom was so mad, she wouldn't let him leave the house for a month...\n\nBut, um... yeah. I don't want to die. I just... I get these thoughts sometimes about not waking up, and it's scary.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in activities for most of the day, every day, for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Loss of interest? I mean, I guess. Like I said before with the TV and the baking show, I just kind of sit there. So yeah, for the past couple of weeks, it's just been a lot of sitting around. I don't really want to do anything. \n\nEven reading, which I usually love. I used to read a book a week. Mostly thrillers, you know? The ones with the crazy twists at the end that you never see coming. I picked up this one book by that author who used to be a homicide detective in Chicago. He writes these incredibly detailed crime scenes because he actually worked them for like twenty years. Which makes sense, I guess. It's like trying to describe the smell of rain on hot pavement. Petrichor, they call it. I learned that word from a crossword puzzle. I used to do the Sunday crossword in pen, which always drove my ex-wife crazy. She said it was arrogant to do a crossword in pen. She was always so particular about things like that. Like, the dishwasher had to be loaded in this exact specific way, or she'd take everything out and redo it...\n\nBut, sorry... what was the question? Oh, yeah. The past two weeks. Yeah, it's been pretty much every day. Just... nothing sounds good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most activities for more than half of the days in the past month?\n\nPatient: The whole month? I mean, I'm not really sure about the entire month. It's hard to keep track of time like that. A month ago was... what, just before Halloween? I didn't even get any trick-or-treaters this year. I bought a huge bag of those miniature candy bars, too. The ones with the mix of Snickers and Twix. Twix is my favorite, but only the left one. Just kidding, I know they're exactly the same, but they had this whole marketing campaign about the left Twix and right Twix a while back. It was actually pretty clever. \n\nMarketing is fascinating to me. I took a business class at the community college years ago, back when I thought I wanted to open my own landscaping company. The professor was this guy who used to work for a big ad agency in New York in the eighties. He always wore these really loud, patterned ties. Like, neon geometric shapes. He said it was to keep our attention, but honestly, it was just distracting. I couldn't stop staring at this one tie that looked like a giant game of Tetris. He also drank Diet Coke constantly. He'd bring a six-pack to a two-hour lecture and just chain-drink them. I always wondered how his stomach handled all that carbonation...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, the past month. Yeah, I don't know about more than half the days for the whole month. Like I said, the last couple weeks have been the main thing. They're just more comfortable, especially when you're bending and lifting a lot. \n\nI work as a personal care aide, so I'm always helping people out of bed or into wheelchairs. He told me once that the secret to a good pastry is the butter. Like, you can't skimp on the butter or it just doesn't flake right. There's this little independent theater downtown that plays old classics and foreign films. Dave's a plumber, and he spent the whole movie complaining about how unrealistic the pipes looked in one of the scenes. He's always doing that, ruining movies by pointing out technical flaws. He's got this massive tool chest that takes up half his garage. It's bright red, like fire engine red. He spent a fortune on it. His wife was so mad when he bought it, she actually made him sleep on the couch for a week. The professor was this guy who used to work for a big ad agency in New York in the eighties. He always wore these really loud, patterned ties. Like, neon geometric shapes. He said it was to keep our attention, but honestly, it was just distracting. I couldn't stop staring at this one tie that looked like a giant game of Tetris. He also drank Diet Coke constantly. He'd bring a six-pack to a two-hour lecture and just chain-drink them. I always wondered how his stomach handled all that carbonation...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, the past month. Yeah, I don't know about more than half the days for the whole month. Like I said, the last couple weeks have been the main thing. Before that, I wasn't really keeping score.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really been paying attention to the scale or anything. I mean, my pants still fit the same. I usually buy those jeans with the little bit of stretch in the waistband anyway. They're just more comfortable, especially when you're bending and lifting a lot. \n\nI work as a personal care aide, so I'm always helping people out of bed or into wheelchairs. It's a lot of physical work. You really have to watch your back. My supervisor, Brenda, she threw her back out last year just trying to pick up a laundry basket. She was out of work for like three months. She had to go to physical therapy twice a week at this clinic over on Elm Street. It's right next to that bakery that makes those amazing bear claws. I used to stop there sometimes before my morning shift. The guy who runs it is from Germany, I think. He has this really thick accent and he's always covered in flour. He told me once that the secret to a good pastry is the butter. Like, you can't skimp on the butter or it just doesn't flake right. He imports his butter from somewhere in Europe, which seems crazy expensive for a little bakery in town, but I guess it works for him because there's always a line out the door on Saturday mornings...\n\nBut, uh, no. I haven't noticed any weight loss. I haven't really been eating much lately, but I haven't dropped any pounds that I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Enjoy doing? Um... no, not really. I can't really think of anything. Like I said, even reading just feels like a chore right now. I used to really like going to the movies, too. There's this little independent theater downtown that plays old classics and foreign films. They have actual real butter for the popcorn, not that weird yellow oil stuff you get at the big chain theaters. \n\nThe seats are kind of lumpy, though. They're that old red velvet material that smells a little bit like dust and old perfume. I went there last year to see this Italian movie about a bicycle thief. It was in black and white, and the subtitles were kind of hard to read because they were white and kept blending into the background. My buddy Dave came with me. Dave's a plumber, and he spent the whole movie complaining about how unrealistic the pipes looked in one of the scenes. He's always doing that, ruining movies by pointing out technical flaws. Like, we watched Die Hard once and he talked for twenty minutes about how the HVAC system in the Nakatomi Plaza made no sense for a building that size in Los Angeles. He actually drew a diagram on a napkin at the diner afterwards to prove his point...\n\nBut yeah, anyway... what did you ask? Oh, pleasure. No, I don't really have anything I'm enjoying right now. It's just all kind of flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling restless and fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Or fidgety? Um, I don't think so. I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, I feel like I'm just moving at my regular pace. \n\nSpeaking of moving, though, I've been meaning to take my car into the shop because it's been vibrating weirdly when I get up to highway speeds. It's an older Subaru, and I know they have those issues with the head gaskets sometimes. My neighbor, Gary, he works on cars in his driveway on the weekends. He's got this massive tool chest that takes up half his garage. It's bright red, like fire engine red. He spent a fortune on it. His wife was so mad when he bought it, she actually made him sleep on the couch for a week. Which is funny, because their couch is this terrible faux leather thing that squeaks every time you shift your weight. I helped them move it in a couple of years ago, and it was so heavy we almost dropped it down the front steps. Gary slipped on some wet leaves, and I swear we missed taking out his mailbox by about two inches... \n\nAnyway, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. Yeah, no, I haven't been fidgety or moving slow or anything. Just normal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your sadness is interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Interfering? Yeah, I guess so. I mean, at work I've had to ask some of my coworkers to double-check my client documentation. You know, making sure I'm writing down the right times for meds and stuff. It's just hard to keep it all straight. \n\nActually, speaking of meds, one of my clients, Mr. Henderson, he takes this blood pressure pill that is the exact same color and shape as a breath mint. It's this tiny little white circle. I almost popped one in my mouth the other day thinking it was a Tic Tac. He keeps them in this old metal tin on his nightstand right next to a bowl of actual mints. It's a disaster waiting to happen. I told his daughter she needs to get him one of those plastic pill organizers with the days of the week on it. The ones with the little pop-up lids. My grandma used to have one of those, but hers had a timer on it that would beep really loud when it was time for her pills. It sounded like a smoke detector. It used to scare her cat half to death. The cat's name was Barnaby, he was this huge orange tabby that weighed like twenty pounds. He loved eating cantaloupe, which is super weird for a cat, right? I tried to give him a piece of tuna once and he just looked at me like I was insulting him, but if you sliced open a melon, he was right there...\n\nBut yeah, anyway, the sadness. It's definitely messing with things. At home, my wife has had to take over paying some of the bills and doing the scheduling because I'm just not as reliable with the details right now. I just kind of forget things or can't focus on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your sadness is present most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day? Yeah, I'd say so. Lately I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I can get through my shift and talk to people, but inside I'm just sad and flat. \n\nSpeaking of my shift, I had to help this new guy, Marcus, figure out the scheduling software yesterday. It's this ancient program that looks like it was built in the nineties. The background is this terrible neon blue color that gives you a headache if you stare at it too long. Marcus is a nice guy, but he's got these huge hands, like catcher's mitts, and he kept hitting two keys at once on the keyboard. We were trying to input the weekend rotation, and he accidentally scheduled himself for a 48-hour shift. I told him he'd need a lot of coffee for that. \n\nI used to drink this specific kind of coffee, a dark roast from Guatemala. I'd buy the whole beans and grind them myself every morning. The grinder I had was a burr grinder, not one of those cheap blade ones that just chops the beans into uneven chunks. My brother-in-law got it for me for Christmas a few years ago. He always buys the most random gifts. One year he got me a subscription to a magazine about amateur beekeeping. I don't even like bees. I got stung by a yellow jacket when I was ten, right on the earlobe, and it swelled up to the size of a golf ball...\n\nBut, uh, anyway... the sadness. Yeah, it's pretty much there all day. Just that heavy feeling, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've had at least five of these symptoms, such as the heavy feeling, lack of interest, or trouble concentrating, nearly every day for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely more than five of those things. That low, guilty, foggy feeling has been hanging over me for about a month now, pretty much every single day. \n\nSpeaking of a month, I think it's been exactly a month since my wife and I tried to go to that new Italian place downtown for our anniversary. We had reservations and everything, but when we got there, the power was out on the whole block. Just completely dark. We ended up getting tacos from this food truck parked near the plaza instead. The guy running the truck had this incredible mustache, like an old-timey bartender, curled up at the ends with wax. He said he uses a special beeswax he orders from a farm in Oregon. I actually visited Oregon once, right out of high school. My buddy Dave and I drove his beat-up Honda Civic all the way to Portland. The car didn't have air conditioning, and we got stuck in traffic in Nevada when it was like a hundred and ten degrees outside. We had to roll the windows down, but all that did was blow hot dust into our faces. Dave had this mix CD playing on a loop, and I swear if I ever hear that one Smash Mouth song again, I might lose my mind. He refused to skip it because the skip button on his stereo was broken and he didn't want to risk jamming the CD player...\n\nBut, uh, yeah, anyway, the symptoms. It's been about a month of feeling like this every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your sadness is severe enough that it's nearly overwhelming at times?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming? No, I wouldn't say overwhelming. It's just... it's heavy, like I said. But I can still manage, mostly. It doesn't feel like it's going to crush me or anything, it's just always there. \n\nAlways there, kind of like that one pothole at the end of my street. I swear, the city has come out to patch it three times this year, and every time it rains, the patch just washes right out. It's right where you have to turn to get onto the main road, so if you don't swing wide, you hit it every single time. My neighbor, Mr. Abernathy, he hit it so hard last week he blew out his front tire. He was so mad. He's this retired postal worker who spends all day tending to his rose bushes. He's got these prize-winning hybrid teas that he talks to. Literally talks to them, like they're people. He told me once that playing classical music helps them grow, so he put this waterproof Bluetooth speaker out in his garden and plays Mozart for them on a loop. I mean, they are beautiful roses, I'll give him that. The red ones are this deep, velvety color, almost like... like a really expensive glass of wine. My wife loves red wine, but it always gives me a headache. We went to a wine tasting for her birthday a couple years ago, out in the country, and I ended up just eating all the fancy cheeses they had out on the patio...\n\nBut, uh, no. The sadness isn't overwhelming. Just... persistent, I guess. Mild, but persistent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your sadness is significantly interfering with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, a little bit. I mean, at work, I've been having to ask my coworkers to double-check my client documentation for me. Charting is so tedious anyway, and we have these new tablets we're supposed to use. They have these really thick, rubbery cases on them so they don't break if a client knocks them off a table. One of the other aides, Sarah, she dropped hers right in a huge puddle in the parking lot last week. It was pouring rain. We've had so much rain lately, my gutters are completely overflowing. I meant to clean them out last fall, but I just never got around to it. You have to get up on this really tall aluminum extension ladder to reach the back of the house, and I hate heights. Ever since I went on a Ferris wheel when I was a teenager at the county fair and it got stuck at the very top for like forty-five minutes. My cousin was rocking the cart back and forth trying to be funny, and I swear I almost threw up my funnel cake... \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, right, responsibilities. Yeah, I'm just not as reliable with details right now. So even at home, my wife has stepped in and taken over paying some of the bills and doing the scheduling. She bought this big dry-erase calendar for the fridge to keep track of it all. It came with these magnetic markers, but the magnets are really cheap so they just slide down the front of the fridge and end up under the stove. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't think so. I mean, I still like doing the things I usually do. Like, I still enjoy watching baseball on the weekends. My team hasn't been doing great this season, though. Their bullpen is just a disaster right now. They brought up this rookie pitcher from Triple-A a couple weeks ago, and the kid throws like a hundred miles an hour, but he has absolutely no control. He walked four guys in one inning on Tuesday. I was watching it with my brother-in-law, Mark. Mark's a huge fan, he actually has season tickets right behind the home dugout. He gets them through his company, they do some kind of corporate sponsorship thing. He works for this logistics firm that ships auto parts all over the Midwest. He was telling me about how they had this massive shipment of brake pads get lost somewhere in Ohio last month. The truck just completely vanished off the GPS tracking system. They thought it got hijacked or something, but it turned out the driver just parked it at a truck stop and quit on the spot because he won the lottery. Not the big jackpot, but like fifty grand on a scratch-off ticket. Can you imagine? Just walking away from your rig like that...

But, uh... yeah, no. I still enjoy my usual stuff. Just... nothing's really changed there.

I'd say this low, guilty, foggy feeling has been hanging over me for about a month now. Yeah, I think it started right around the time I had to take my car in for its annual inspection. I drive this older Civic, and the mechanic tried to tell me I needed new brake pads, which is just crazy because I changed them myself last summer. I used to do a lot of my own car work back when I had a real driveway. \n\nMy buddy Dave and I actually spent a whole weekend once replacing the alternator on his old Ford Ranger. We ordered pizza from this local place that makes the crust super thick, almost like a deep dish but not quite. Dave always gets pineapple and jalapeño on his, which sounds absolutely terrible but it actually kind of works if you dip it in ranch dressing. They used to give us these little plastic cups of homemade ranch that were just... so good. \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the timeline. But yeah, it's been about a month of just feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess I just don't really have the energy for much of anything lately. Like, I used to love going to the flea market on Sunday mornings. You can find the craziest stuff there. Last year I found this incredible cast-iron skillet, Griswold brand, from like the 1930s. It was completely rusted over, but I spent weeks re-seasoning it. You have to use flaxseed oil and bake it at exactly 450 degrees for an hour, let it cool in the oven, and do that like six times. My grandma used to have a skillet just like it. She made the best cornbread in it, the kind with actual corn kernels mixed into the batter. She always said the secret was heating the pan with bacon grease before you pour the batter in, so the edges get all crispy... \n\nBut, um... yeah, I haven't been to the flea market in weeks. I just don't feel like it. I don't really feel like doing much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really... It's basically just edible Play-Doh. \n\nMy cousin Sarah had a wedding cake completely covered in it, and I remember everyone just peeling it off and leaving it in these little piles on their plates. Our dog keeps trying to bat them around with his paws... but anyway, yeah, she's handling the details for now because I'm just slipping up on them.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I still like doing the things I usually do. Like, I still enjoy watching baseball on the weekends. My team hasn't been doing great this season, though. Their bullpen is just a disaster right now. They brought up this rookie pitcher from Triple-A a couple weeks ago, and the kid throws like a hundred miles an hour, but he has absolutely no control. He walked four guys in one inning on Tuesday. I was watching it with my brother-in-law, Mark. Mark's a huge fan, he actually has season tickets right behind the home dugout. He gets them through his company, they do some kind of corporate sponsorship thing. He works for this logistics firm that ships auto parts all over the Midwest. He was telling me about how they had this massive shipment of brake pads get lost somewhere in Ohio last month. The truck just completely vanished off the GPS tracking system. They thought it got hijacked or something, but it turned out the driver just parked it at a truck stop and quit on the spot because he won the lottery. Not the big jackpot, but like fifty grand on a scratch-off ticket. Can you imagine? Just walking away from your rig like that...\n\nBut, uh... yeah, no. I still enjoy my usual stuff. Just... nothing's really changed there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current state is consistent with a mild depressive episode or something more severe?\n\nPatient: Well, I'd say it's mild. Definitely mild. Like I said, it's just this heavy feeling, but it's not stopping me from getting out of bed or anything. My bed actually needs a new mattress, now that I think about it. The springs on my side are completely shot. I wake up with this crick in my neck almost every morning. We bought it from this discount warehouse place out by the highway, the one with the giant inflatable gorilla on the roof? They had this huge Presidents' Day sale, and the salesman—guy named Phil, wore way too much cologne, smelled like cheap pine needles—he swore it was top of the line. Said it was the same brand they use in five-star hotels. I don't know what kind of hotels he's staying in, but it feels like sleeping on a bag of walnuts. My wife wants to get one of those memory foam ones, the ones that come rolled up in a box. I saw a video online of a guy unboxing one, and it just expanded like a giant marshmallow. He actually set it up in his living room and his cat wouldn't stop attacking it... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. To answer your question, it's just a mild thing. Not severe at all, just... dragging a bit."
